Decidability for Entailments of Symbolic Heaps with Arrays
Daisuke Kimura \rsuper a, Makoto Tatsuta \rsuper b

Abstract
. This paper presents two decidability results on the validity checking problem for entailments of symbolic heaps in separation logic with Presburger arithmetic and arrays. The first result is for a system with arrays and existential quantifiers. The correctness of the decision procedure is proved under the condition that sizes of arrays in the succedent are not existentially quantified. This condition is different from that proposed by Brotherston et al. in 2017 and one of them does not imply the other. The main idea is a novel translation from an entailment of symbolic heaps into a formula in Presburger arithmetic. The second result is the decidability for a system with both arrays and lists. The key idea is to extend the unroll collapse technique proposed by Berdine et al. in 2005 to arrays and arithmetic as well as double-linked lists.



Feb. 19, 2018May 11, 2021

†
†
lmcs-doi:
Volume 17, Issue 2, Paper 15

1.
Introduction

Separation logic
[
20
]
has been successfully used to verify/analyze heap-manipulating imperative programs with pointers

[
3
,
7
,
8
,
9
,
12
]
,
and in particular it is successful for verify/analyze memory safety.
The advantage of separation logic is modularity brought by the frame rule,
with which we can independently verify/analyze each function that may
manipulate heaps
[
9
]
.

In order to develop an automated analyzer/verifier of pointer programs based on separation logic,
symbolic-heap systems, which are fragments of separation logic,
are often considered
[
2
,
3
,
9
]
.
Despite of its simple and restricted form, symbolic heaps have enough expressive power, for example,
Infer
[
10
]
and HIP/SLEEK
are based on symbolic-heap systems.
Symbolic heaps
are used as assertions
A
and
B
in Hoare-triple

\{A\}P\{B\}
.
For program analysis/verification,
the validity of entailments need to be checked automatically.

Inductive definitions in a symbolic-heap system is important,
since they can describe recursive data structures such as lists and trees.
Symbolic-heap systems with inductive predicates have been studied intensively
[
2
,
3
,
1
,
6
,
11
,
13
,
14
,
15
,
16
,
21
]
.
Berdine et al.
[
2
,
3
]
introduced a symbolic-heap system with hard-coded list and tree predicates, and showed the decidability of its entailment problem. Iosif et al.
[
15
,
16
]
considered a system with general inductive predicates, and showed its decidability under the bounded tree-width condition. Tatsuta et al.
[
21
]
introduced a system with general monadic inductive predicates.

Arrays are one of the most important primitive data structures of programs.
It is also important to verify that there is no buffer overflow in programs with arrays.
In order to verify/analyze pointer programs with arrays,
this paper introduces two symbolic-heap systems with the array predicate.

The first symbolic-heap system, called
SLA
(Separation Logic with Arrays), contains the points-to and the array predicates as well as existential quantifiers in spatial parts.
The entailments of this system also have disjunction in the succedents.
Our first main theorem is the decidability of the entailment problem.
For this theorem we need the condition:
the sizes of arrays in the succedent of an entailment do not contain any existential variables.
It means that the size of arrays in the succedent is completely determined by the antecedent.

The basic idea of our decision procedure for
SLA
is a novel translation of a given entailment into an equivalent formula in Presburger arithmetic.
We use “
sorted
” symbolic heaps as a key idea for defining the translation.
A heap represented by a sorted symbolic heap has addresses sorted in the order of the spatial part.
If both sides of a given entailment are sorted,
the validity of the entailment is decided
by comparing spatial parts on both sides starting from left to right.

The second system, called
SLAL
(Separation Logic with Arrays and Lists), contains the points-to predicate, the array predicate, the singly-linked list predicate, and the doubly-linked list predicate.
They also have disjunction in the succedents.
The entailments of this system are restricted to quantifier-free symbolic heaps.
This restriction comes from a technical reason, namely, our key idea (the unroll collapse technique) does not work in the presence of existential quantifiers.
Although the restriction reduces the expressive power of entailments, the quantifier-free entailments are useful for verify/analyze memory safety
[
2
,
3
]
.

The second main theorem of this paper is the decidability of the entailment problem for
SLAL
.
Our decision procedure is split into the two stages (a) and (b):
(a) the first stage eliminates the list predicates from
the antecedent of a given entailment
by applying
the unroll collapse technique
.
Originally the unroll collapse for acyclic singly-linked list segments is
invented by Berdine et al.
[
2
]
.
We extend the original one in two ways;
the unroll collapse in
SLAL
is extended to arithmetic and arrays
as well as doubly-linked list segments.
(b) the second stage eliminates list predicates from
the succedent of the entailment by the proof search technique.
To do this, we introduce a sound and complete proof system that has valid quantifier-free entailments in
SLA
as axioms,
which are checked by the first decision procedure for
SLA
.

As related work, as far as we know there are
two papers about symbolic-heap systems that have arrays as primitive.
Calcagno et al.
[
8
]
studied program analysis based on a symbolic-heap system in the presence of pointer arithmetic.
They assumed a decision procedure for entailments with arrays and did not propose it.
Brotherston et al.
[
7
]
considered the same system as
SLA
, and investigated several problems about it.

In
[
7
]
, they proposed a decision procedure for the entailment problem (of
SLA
) by
giving an equivalent condition to the existence of a counter-model for a given entailment, then
checking a Presburger formula that expresses the condition.
In order to do this, they imposed the restriction that the second argument of
the points-to predicate in the succedent of an entailment is not existentially quantified.
Their result decides a different class of entailments from the class decided by ours, and
our class neither contains their class nor is contained by it.

When we extend separation logic with arrays, it may be different from previous array
logics in the points that (1) it is specialized for memory safety, and
(2) it can scale up by modularity.
Bradley et al.
[
5
]
, Bouajjani et al.
[
4
]
, and Lahiri et al.
[
18
]

discussed logics for arrays but their systems are totally
different from separation logic. So we cannot apply their techniques to our
case.
Piskac et al.
[
19
]
proposed a separation logic system with list
segments, and it can be combined with various SMT solvers, including
array logics. However, when we combine it with array logics, the arrays
are external and the resulting system does not describe the arrays by
spatial formulas with separating conjunction.

The first result of this paper is based on
[
17
]
but
we give detailed proofs of the key lemmas (stated in Lemma
4
) for the result.

The paper is organized as follows.
Section 2 introduces the first system
SLA
.
Section 3 defines and discusses the decision procedure of the entailment problem for
SLA
.
In Section 4,
we show the first main theorem, namely, the decidability result of
SLA
.
In Section 5, we introduce the second system
SLAL
.
Section 6 shows the unroll collapse property.
Section 7 gives a decision procedure for
SLAL
.
Section 8 shows the second main theorem, namely, the decidability result of
SLAL
.
We conclude in Section 9.

2.
Separation Logic with Arrays

This section defines the syntax and semantics of our separation logic with arrays.
We first give the separation logic
G
with arrays in the ordinary style.
Then we define the symbolic-heap system
SLA
as a fragment of
G
.

2.1.
Syntax of System
G
of Separation Logic with Arrays

We use the following notations in this paper.
Let
(p_{j})_{j\in J}
be a sequence indexed by a finite set
J
.
We write
\{p_{j}\ |\ j\in J\}
for this sequence.
This sequence will sometimes be abbreviated by
\overrightarrow{p}
.
We write
q\in\overrightarrow{p}
when
q
is an element of
\overrightarrow{p}
.

We have first-order variables
x,y,z,\ldots\in\hbox{Vars}
and constants
0,1,2,\ldots
.
The syntax of
G
is defined as follows:

Terms

\displaystyle t::=x\ |\ 0\ |\ 1\ |\ 2\ |\cdots|\ t+t.

Formulas

\displaystyle F::=t=t\ |\ F\land F\ |\ \neg F\ |\ \exists xF\ |\ \hbox{Emp}\ |\ t\mapsto(t,\ldots,t)\ |\ {\rm Arr}(t,t)\ |\ F*F.

Atomic formulas
t\mapsto(u_{1},\ldots,u_{{\it pt}})
and
{\rm Arr}(t,u)
are called
a points-to atomic formula and an array atomic formula, respectively.
The points-to predicate
\mapsto
is
({\it pt}+1)
-ary predicate
(the number
{\it pt}
is fixed beforehand).
We sometimes write
t\mapsto u
instead of
t\mapsto(u)
when
\mapsto
is a binary predicate.
We use the symbol
-
to denote an unspecified term.

Each formula is interpreted by a variable assignment and a heap:

Emp
is true when the heap is empty;

t\mapsto(\overrightarrow{u})
is true when
the heap has only a single memory cell of address
t
that contains the value
\overrightarrow{u}
;

{\rm Arr}(t,u)
is true when the heap has only an array
of index from
t
to
u
;
a separating conjunction
F_{1}*F_{2}
is true when
the heap is split into some two disjoint sub-heaps,

F_{1}
is true for one, and
F_{2}
is true for the other.
The formal definition of these interpretations is given in the next subsection.

A term
t
that appears in either
t\mapsto(\hbox{-})
,
{\rm Arr}(t,\hbox{-})
or
{\rm Arr}(\hbox{-},t)
of
F

is called an address term of
F
.
The set of free variables (denoted by
\hbox{FV}(F)
) of
F
is defined as usual.
We also define
\hbox{FV}(\overrightarrow{F})
as the union of
\hbox{FV}(F)
, where
F\in\overrightarrow{F}
.

We use abbreviations
F_{1}\vee F_{2}
,
F_{1}\to F_{2}
, and
\forall xF
defined in the usual way.
We also write
t\neq u
,
t\leq u
,
t<u
,
True
, and
False

for
\neg(t=u)
,
\exists x(u=t+x)
,
t+1\leq u
,

0=0
, and
0\neq 0
, respectively.

A formula is said to be
pure
if it
is a formula of Presburger arithmetic.

We implicitly use the associative and commutative laws for the connectives
*
and
\land
,
the fact that
Emp
is the unit of
*
and
True
is the unit of
\land
,
and permutation of the existential quantifiers.

For
I
a finite set and
\{F_{i}\}_{i\in I}
a set of formulas,
we write
\bigwedge_{i\in I}F_{i}
for the conjunction of the elements of
\{F_{i}\}_{i\in I}
.
If
I
is empty, it is defined as
True
.

2.2.
Semantics of System
G
of Separation Logic with Arrays

Let
N
be the set of natural numbers. We define the following semantic domains:

\begin{array}[]{l}\hbox{Val}\overset{{\rm def}}{=}N,\quad\hbox{Loc}\overset{{\rm def}}{=}N\setminus\{0\},\quad{\rm Store}\overset{{\rm def}}{=}\hbox{Vars}\to\hbox{Val},\quad{\rm Heap}\overset{{\rm def}}{=}\hbox{Loc}\to_{\rm fin}\hbox{Val}^{\it pt}.\end{array}

Loc
means addresses of heaps.
0
means Null.
An element
s
in
{\rm Store}
is called a
store

that means a valuation of variables.
The update
s[x_{1}:=a_{1},\ldots,x_{k}:=a_{k}]
of
s
is defined by

s[x_{1}:=a_{1},\ldots,x_{k}:=a_{k}](z)=a_{i}
if
z=x_{i}
,
otherwise
s[x_{1}:=a_{1},\ldots,x_{k}:=a_{k}](z)=s(z)
.
An element
h
in
{\rm Heap}
is called a
heap
.
The domain of
h
(denoted by
{\rm Dom}(h)
) means the memory addresses which are currently used.

h(n)
means the value at the address
n
if it is defined.
We sometimes use notation
h_{1}+h_{2}
for the disjoint union of
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
,
that is, it is defined when
{\rm Dom}(h_{1})
and
{\rm Dom}(h_{2})
are disjoint sets, and

(h_{1}+h_{2})(n)
is
h_{i}(n)
if
n\in{\rm Dom}(h_{i})
for
i=1,2
.
The restriction
h|_{X}
of
h
is defined by
{\rm Dom}(h|_{X})=X\cap{\rm Dom}(h)

and
h|_{X}(m)=h(m)
for any
m\in{\rm Dom}(h|_{X})
.
A pair
(s,h)
is called a
heap model
.

The interpretation
s(t)
of a term
t
by
s
is defined by
extending the definition of
s
by
s(n)=n
for each constant
n
,
and
s(t+u)=s(t)+s(u)
.
We also use the notation
s(\overrightarrow{u})
defined by
s(u_{1},\ldots,u_{k})=(s(u_{1}),\ldots,s(u_{k}))
.

The interpretation
s,h\models F
of
F
under the heap model
(s,h)

is defined inductively as follows:

s,h\models t=u

iff
s(t)=s(u)
,

s,h\models F_{1}\land F_{2}

iff
s,h\models F_{1}
and
s,h\models F_{2}
,

s,h\models\neg F

iff
s,h\not\models F
,

s,h\models\exists xF

iff
s[x:=a],h\models F
for some
a\in\hbox{Val}
,

s,h\models\hbox{Emp}

iff
{\rm Dom}(h)=\emptyset
,

s,h\models t\mapsto(\overrightarrow{u})

iff
{\rm Dom}(h)=\{s(t)\}
and
h(s(t))=s(\overrightarrow{u})
,

s,h\models{\rm Arr}(t,u)

iff
s(t)\leq s(u)
and
{\rm Dom}(h)=\{x\in N\ |\ s(t)\leq x\leq s(u)\}
,

s,h\models F_{1}*F_{2}

iff
s,h_{1}\models F_{1}
,
s,h_{2}\models F_{2}
, and
h=h_{1}+h_{2}
for some
h_{1}
,
h_{2}
.

We sometimes write
s\models F
if
s,h\models F
holds for any
h
.
This notation is mainly used for pure formulas,
since their interpretation does not depend on the heap-part of heap models.
We also write
\models F
if
s,h\models F
holds for any
s
and
h
.

The notation
F_{1}\models F_{2}
is an abbreviation of
\models F_{1}\to F_{2}
, that is,
s,h\models F_{1}
implies
s,h\models F_{2}
for any
s
and
h
.

2.3.
Symbolic-Heap System with Arrays

The symbolic-heap system
SLA
is defined as a fragment of
G
.
The syntax of
SLA
is given as follows.
Terms of
SLA
are the same as those of
G
.
Formulas of
SLA
(called
symbolic heaps
) have the following form:

\begin{array}[]{l}\phi::=\exists\overrightarrow{x}(\Pi\land\Sigma)\end{array}

where
\Pi
is a pure formula of
G

and
\Sigma
is the spatial part defined by

\begin{array}[]{l}\Sigma::=\hbox{Emp}\ |\ t\mapsto(t,\ldots,t)\ |\ {\rm Arr}(t,t)\ |\ \Sigma*\Sigma.\end{array}

In this paper, we use the following notations for symbolic heaps.
The symbol
\sigma
is used to denote an atomic formula of
\Sigma
.
We write
x\mapsto\_
for

\exists z(x\mapsto z)
where
z
is fresh.
We also write
\exists\overrightarrow{x}(\Pi\land\Sigma)\land\Pi^{\prime}
for
\exists\overrightarrow{x}(\Pi\land\Pi^{\prime}\land\Sigma)
,
write
\exists\overrightarrow{x}(\Pi\land\Sigma)*\Sigma^{\prime}
for
\exists\overrightarrow{x}(\Pi\land\Sigma*\Sigma^{\prime})
,
and
write
\exists\overrightarrow{x}(\Pi\land\Sigma)*\exists\overrightarrow{x^{\prime}}(\Pi^{\prime}\land\Sigma^{\prime})
for

\exists\overrightarrow{x}\overrightarrow{x^{\prime}}(\Pi\land\Pi^{\prime}\land\Sigma*\Sigma^{\prime})
.

In this paper, we consider
entailments
of
SLA
that have the form:

\begin{array}[]{l}\phi\mathrel{\vdash}\{\phi_{i}\ |\ i\in I\}\qquad\hbox{($I$ is a finite set)}.\end{array}

The left-hand side of the symbol
\mathrel{\vdash}
is called the antecedent.
The right-hand side of the symbol
\mathrel{\vdash}
is called the succedent.
The right-hand side
\{\phi_{i}\ |\ i\in I\}
of an entailment means the disjunction of the symbolic heaps
\phi_{i}
(
i\in I
).

An entailment
\phi\mathrel{\vdash}\{\phi_{i}\ |\ i\in I\}
is said to be
valid

if

\phi\models\bigvee\{\phi_{i}\ |\ i\in I\}
holds.

A formula of the form
\Pi\land\Sigma
is called a
QF symbolic heap
(denoted by
\varphi
).
Note that existential quantifiers may appear in the pure part of a QF symbolic heap.
We can easily see that

\exists\overrightarrow{x}\varphi\models\overrightarrow{\phi}
is equivalent to

\varphi\models\overrightarrow{\phi}
.
So we often assume that the left-hand sides of entailments are QF symbolic heaps.

We call entailments of the form
\varphi\mathrel{\vdash}\{\varphi_{i}\ |\ i\in I\}

QF entailments
.

2.4.
Analysis/Verification of Memory Safety

We intend to use our entailment checker as a part of our
analysis/verification system for memory safety.
We briefly explain it for motivating our entailment checker.

The target programming language is essentially the same as
that in
[
20
]
except we extend the allocation command
{\tt malloc}
,
which returns the first address of the allocated memory block or
returns
nil
if it fails to allocate.
We define our programming language in programming language C style.

Expressions

\displaystyle e::=x\ |\ 0\ |\ 1\ |\ 2\ldots\ |\ e+e.

Boolean expressions

\displaystyle b::=e==e\ |\ e<e\ |\ b\&\&b\ |\ b\|b\ |\ !b.

Programs

\displaystyle P::=x=e;\ |\ {\tt if}\ (b)\{P\}\ {\tt else}\ \{P\};\ |\ {\tt while}\ (b)\{P\};\ |\ P\ P

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\ |\ x={\tt malloc}(y);\ |\ x=*y;\ |\ *x=y;\ |\ {\tt free}(x);.

x={\tt malloc}(y);
allocates
y
cells and set
x
to
the pointer to the first cell. Note that this operation may fail
if there is not enough free memory.

Our assertion language is a disjunction of symbolic heaps, namely,

\begin{array}[]{l}\hbox{Assertions}\quad A::=\phi_{1}\lor\dots\lor\phi_{n}.\end{array}

We write
\phi*(\phi_{1}\lor\ldots\lor\phi_{n})
for

(\phi*\phi_{1}\lor\ldots\lor\phi*\phi_{n})
, and
write
\exists x(\phi_{1}\lor\ldots\lor\phi_{n})
for

(\exists x\phi_{1}\lor\ldots\lor\exists x\phi_{n})
.
The notation
\Pi\land(\phi_{1}\lor\ldots\lor\phi_{n})
is defined similarly.

In the same way as
[
20
]
,
we use a triple
\{{A}\}\hskip 2.15277pt{P}\hskip 2.15277pt\{{B}\}
that means that
if the assertion
A
holds at the initial state and the program
P

is executed, then (1) if
P
terminates then the assertion
B
holds
at the resulting state, and (2)
P
does not cause any memory errors.

As inference rules for triples,
we have ordinary inference rules for Hoare triples including the consequence
rule, as well as the following rules (axioms) for memory operations.
We write
{\rm Arr}2(x,y)
for
\exists z({\rm Arr}(x,z)\land x+y=z+1)
.

{\rm Arr}2(x,y)
denotes the memory block at address
x
of size
y
.

\begin{array}[]{l}\{{A}\}\hskip 2.15277pt{x={\tt malloc}(y);}\hskip 2.15277pt\{{\exists x^{\prime}(A[x:=x^{\prime}]\land x=\hbox{nil}\lor A[x:=x^{\prime}]*{\rm Arr}2(x,y[x:=x^{\prime}]))}\},\\
\{{A*y\mapsto t}\}\hskip 2.15277pt{x=*y;}\hskip 2.15277pt\{{\exists x^{\prime}(A[x:=x^{\prime}]*y\mapsto t[x:=x^{\prime}]\land x=t[x:=x^{\prime}])}\},\\
\{{\exists x^{\prime}(A*x\mapsto x^{\prime})}\}\hskip 2.15277pt{*x=y;}\hskip 2.15277pt\{{A*x\mapsto y}\},\\
\{{\exists x^{\prime}(A*x\mapsto x^{\prime})}\}\hskip 2.15277pt{{\tt free}(x);}\hskip 2.15277pt\{{A}\},\ \hbox{where $x^{\prime}$ is fresh}.\end{array}

In order to prove memory safety of a program
P
under a precondition
A
,
it is sufficient to
show that

\{A\}P\{\hbox{True}\}
is provable.

By separation logic with arrays, we can show a triple

\{{A}\}\hskip 2.15277pt{x={\tt malloc}(y);}\hskip 2.15277pt\{{\exists x^{\prime}(A[x:=x^{\prime}]\land x=\hbox{nil}\lor A[x:=x^{\prime}]*{\rm Arr}2(x,y[x:=x^{\prime}]))}\}
,
but
it is impossible without arrays since
y
in
{\tt malloc}(y)
is a variable.
With separation logic with arrays,
we can also show

\{{{\rm Arr}(p,p+3)}\}\hskip 2.15277pt{*p=5;}\hskip 2.15277pt\{{p\mapsto 5*{\rm Arr}(p+1,p+3)}\}
.

For the consequence rule

\begin{array}[]{l}\{{A}\}\hskip 2.15277pt{P}\hskip 2.15277pt\{{B}\}\{{A^{\prime}}\}\hskip 2.15277pt{P}\hskip 2.15277pt\{{B^{\prime}}\}\qquad\hbox{(if $A\mathbin{\to}A^{\prime}$ and $B^{\prime}\mathbin{\to}B$)}\end{array}

we have to check the side conditions
A\mathbin{\to}A^{\prime}
and
B^{\prime}\mathbin{\to}B
.
Let
A
be
\phi_{1}\lor\ldots\lor\phi_{n}
and

A^{\prime}
be
\phi^{\prime}_{1}\lor\ldots\lor\phi^{\prime}_{m}
.
Then we will use our entailment checker to decide

\phi_{i}\mathrel{\vdash}\phi^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\phi^{\prime}_{m}
for all
1\leq i\leq n
.

3.
Decision Procedure for
SLA

This section gives our decision procedure of the entailment problem for
SLA

by introducing the key idea, namely sorted symbolic heaps,
for the decision procedure, and defining the translation from sorted symbolic heaps
into formulas in Presburger arithmetic.
We finally state the decidability result for
SLA
,
which is the first main theorem in this paper.

3.1.
Sorted Entailments

This subsection describes
sorted
symbolic heaps.
In this and the next sections,
for simplicity, we assume that the number
{\it pt}
is
1
, that is,
the points-to predicate is a binary one.
The decidability result and the decision procedure in these sections
can be straightforwardly extended to arbitrary
{\it pt}
.
In these two sections,
we will not implicitly use
the commutative law for
*
, or the unit law for
Emp

in order to define the following notations.

We give a pure formula
t<\Sigma
,
which means the first address expressed by
\Sigma
is greater than
t
.
It is inductively defined as follows:

t<\hbox{Emp}\overset{{\rm def}}{=}\hbox{True}
,

t<t_{1}\mapsto(\hbox{-})\overset{{\rm def}}{=}t<t_{1}
,

t<{\rm Arr}(t_{1},\hbox{-})\overset{{\rm def}}{=}t<t_{1}
,

t<(\hbox{Emp}*\Sigma_{1})\overset{{\rm def}}{=}t<\Sigma_{1}
,

t<(t_{1}\mapsto(\hbox{-})*\Sigma_{1})\overset{{\rm def}}{=}t<t_{1}
,

t<({\rm Arr}(t_{1},\hbox{-})*\Sigma_{1})\overset{{\rm def}}{=}t<t_{1}
.

Then we inductively define a pure formula
{\rm Sorted}(\Sigma)
,
which means the address terms are sorted in
\Sigma
as follows.

\displaystyle{\rm Sorted}^{\prime}

\displaystyle(\hbox{Emp})\overset{{\rm def}}{=}\hbox{True},

\displaystyle{\rm Sorted}^{\prime}

\displaystyle(t\mapsto u)\overset{{\rm def}}{=}\hbox{True},

\displaystyle{\rm Sorted}^{\prime}

\displaystyle({\rm Arr}(t,u))\overset{{\rm def}}{=}t\leq u,

\displaystyle{\rm Sorted}^{\prime}

\displaystyle(\hbox{Emp}*\Sigma_{1})\overset{{\rm def}}{=}{\rm Sorted}^{\prime}(\Sigma_{1}),

\displaystyle{\rm Sorted}^{\prime}

\displaystyle(t\mapsto u*\Sigma_{1})\overset{{\rm def}}{=}t<\Sigma_{1}\land{\rm Sorted}^{\prime}(\Sigma_{1}),

\displaystyle{\rm Sorted}^{\prime}

\displaystyle({\rm Arr}(t,u)*\Sigma_{1})\overset{{\rm def}}{=}t\leq u\land u<\Sigma_{1}\land{\rm Sorted}^{\prime}(\Sigma_{1}),

\displaystyle{\rm Sorted}

\displaystyle(\Sigma)\overset{{\rm def}}{=}0<\Sigma\land{\rm Sorted}^{\prime}(\Sigma).

We define
\Sigma^{\sim}
as
{\rm Sorted}(\Sigma)\land\Sigma
.
Let
\phi
be
\exists\vec{x}(\Pi\land\Sigma)
.
We write
\tilde{\phi}
or
\phi^{\sim}
for

\exists\vec{x}(\Pi\land\Sigma^{\sim})
.
We call
\tilde{\phi}
a
sorted symbolic heap
.

We define
{\rm Perm}(\Sigma)
as the set of permutations of
\Sigma
with respect to
*
.
A symbolic heap
\phi^{\prime}
is called a permutation of
\phi
if

\phi=\exists\overrightarrow{x}(\Pi\land\Sigma)
,

\phi^{\prime}=\exists\overrightarrow{x}(\Pi\land\Sigma^{\prime})
and

\Sigma^{\prime}
is a permutation of
\Sigma
.
We write
{\rm Perm}(\phi)
for the set of permutations of
\phi
.
Note that

s,h\models\tilde{\phi^{\prime}}
for some
\phi^{\prime}\in{\rm Perm}(\phi)

if and only if

s,h\models\phi
.
An entailment is said to be
sorted
if all symbolic heaps in its antecedent and succedent are sorted.

The next lemma claims that checking the validity of entailments can be reduced
to checking the validity of sorted entailments.

Lemma 1
.

s\models\tilde{\varphi^{\prime}}\to\displaystyle\mathop{\bigvee}\{\tilde{\phi^{\prime}}\ |\ i\in I,\phi^{\prime}\in{\rm Perm}(\phi_{i})\}

for all
\varphi^{\prime}\in{\rm Perm}(\varphi)

if and only if

s\models\varphi\to\bigvee_{i\in I}\phi_{i}
.

Proof 3.1
.

We first show the left-to-right part.
Assume the left-hand side of the claim.
Fix
\varphi^{\prime}\in{\rm Perm}(\varphi)
and suppose
s,h\models\tilde{\varphi^{\prime}}
.
Then we have
s,h\models\varphi
.
By the assumption,
s,h\models\phi_{i}
for some
i\in I
.
Hence we have
s,h\models\displaystyle\mathop{\bigvee}\{\tilde{\phi^{\prime}}\ |\ i\in I,\phi^{\prime}\in{\rm Perm}(\phi_{i})\}
.
Next we show the right-to-left part.
Assume the right-hand side and
s,h\models\varphi
.
We have
s,h\models\tilde{\varphi^{\prime}}
for some
\varphi^{\prime}\in{\rm Perm}(\varphi)
.
By the assumption,
s,h\models\tilde{\phi^{\prime}}
for some
\phi^{\prime}\in{\rm Perm}(\phi_{i})
.
Thus we have
s,h\models\phi_{i}
for some
i\in I
.

The basic idea of our decision procedure is as follows:
(1) A given entailment is decomposed into sorted entailments according to Lemma
1
;
(2) the decomposed sorted entailments are translated into Presburger formulas by the translation
P
given in the next subsection;
(3) the translated formulas are decided by the decision procedure of Presburger arithmetic.

3.2.
Translation
P

We define the translation
P
from QF entailments into Presburger formulas.
We note that the resulting formula may contain new fresh variables (denoted by
z
).
In the definition of
P
, we fix a linear order on an index set
I

to take an element of the minimum index.
For saving space, we use some auxiliary notations.
Let
\{t_{j}\}_{j\in J}
be a set of terms indexed by a finite set
J
.
We write
u=t_{J}
for
\bigwedge_{j\in J}u=t_{j}
.
We also write
u<t_{J}
for
\bigwedge_{j\in J}u<t_{j}
.
We note that both
u=t_{\emptyset}
and
u<t_{\emptyset}
are
True
,
since
\bigwedge_{j\in\emptyset}u=t_{j}
and
\bigwedge_{j\in\emptyset}u<t_{j}
are defined by
True
.

The definition of
P(\Pi,\Sigma,S)
is given as listed in Fig.
1
, where
S
is a finite set
\{(\Pi_{i},\Sigma_{i})\}_{i\in I}
.
We assume that pattern-matching is done from the top to the bottom.

In order to describe the procedure
P
,
we temporarily extend terms to include
u-t

where
u,t
are terms.
In the result of
P
, which is a
Presburger arithmetic formula,
we eliminate these extended terms by replacing

t^{\prime}+(u-t)=t^{\prime\prime}
and
t^{\prime}+(u-t)<t^{\prime\prime}

by

t^{\prime}+u=t^{\prime\prime}+t
and
t^{\prime}+u<t^{\prime\prime}+t
, respectively.

Let
\sharp_{*}(\Sigma)
be the number of
*
in
\Sigma
.
Let
\sharp_{*}(\{(\Pi_{i},\Sigma_{i})\}_{i\in I})
be

\sum_{i\in I}\sharp_{*}(\Sigma_{i})
.
We define
{\rm FirstRemove}_{\Sigma}(\Sigma^{\prime})
by

\Sigma^{\prime}_{0}
if
\Sigma
has the form
t\mapsto u*\Sigma_{0}
and

\Sigma^{\prime}
has the form
t^{\prime}\mapsto u^{\prime}*\Sigma^{\prime}_{0}
, or

\Sigma^{\prime}
otherwise.
This is obtained as follows:
the points-to predicates at the first positions of
\Sigma
and
\Sigma^{\prime}

are removed (if they exist), then the resulting formula made
from
\Sigma^{\prime}
is returned.

{\rm FirstRemove}_{\Sigma}(\{(\Pi_{i},\Sigma_{i})\}_{i\in I})
is also defined by

\{(\Pi_{i},{\rm FirstRemove}_{\Sigma}(\Sigma_{i}))\}_{i\in I}
.

The
\overset{{\rm def}}{=}
steps terminate
since
the measure
(\sharp_{*}({\rm FirstRemove}_{\Sigma}(S)),\sharp_{*}(\Sigma)+\sharp_{*}(S),|S|)

for
P(\Pi,\Sigma,S)
strictly decreases,
where
|S|
is the number of elements in
S
.

\begin{array}[]{lllr}P(\Pi,\hbox{Emp}*\Sigma,S)&\overset{{\rm def}}{=}&P(\Pi,\Sigma,S)&\hskip 75.0pt{\bf(EmpL)}\\
P(\Pi,\Sigma,\{(\Pi^{\prime},\hbox{Emp}*\Sigma^{\prime})\}\cup S)&\overset{{\rm def}}{=}&P(\Pi,\Sigma,\{(\Pi^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})\}\cup S)&{\bf(EmpR)}\\
P(\Pi,\hbox{Emp},\{(\Pi^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})\}\cup S)&\overset{{\rm def}}{=}&P(\Pi,\hbox{Emp},S),\hskip 9.24994pt\hbox{where $\Sigma^{\prime}\not\equiv\hbox{Emp}$}&{\bf(EmpNEmp)}\\
P(\Pi,\hbox{Emp},\{(\Pi_{i},\hbox{Emp})\}_{i\in I})&\overset{{\rm def}}{=}&\Pi\mathbin{\to}\bigvee_{i\in I}\Pi_{i}&{\bf(EmpEmp)}\\
P(\Pi,\Sigma,\{(\Pi^{\prime},\hbox{Emp})\}\cup S)&\overset{{\rm def}}{=}&P(\Pi,\Sigma,S),\hskip 18.49988pt\hbox{where $\Sigma\not\equiv\hbox{Emp}$}&{\bf(NEmpEmp)}\\
P(\Pi,\Sigma,\emptyset)&\overset{{\rm def}}{=}&\neg(\Pi\land{\rm Sorted}(\Sigma))&{\bf(empty)}\end{array}

P(\Pi,t\mapsto u*\Sigma,\{(\Pi_{i},t_{i}\mapsto u_{i}*\Sigma_{i})\}_{i\in I})

(
\mapsto\mapsto
)

\overset{{\rm def}}{=}

P(\Pi\land t<\Sigma,\Sigma,\{(\Pi_{i}\land t=t_{i}\land u=u_{i}\land t_{i}<\Sigma_{i},\Sigma_{i})\}_{i\in I})

P(\Pi,t\mapsto u*\Sigma,\{(\Pi_{i},{\rm Arr}(t_{i},t_{i}^{\prime})*\Sigma_{i})\}\cup S)

(
\mapsto
Arr)

\overset{{\rm def}}{=}

P(\Pi\land t_{i}^{\prime}=t_{i},t\mapsto u*\Sigma,\{(\Pi_{i},t_{i}\mapsto u*\Sigma_{i})\}\cup S)

\land\ P(\Pi\land t_{i}^{\prime}>t_{i},t\mapsto u*\Sigma,\{(\Pi_{i},t_{i}\mapsto u*{\rm Arr}(t_{i}+1,t_{i}^{\prime})*\Sigma_{i})\}\cup S)

\land\ P(\Pi\land t_{i}^{\prime}<t_{i},t\mapsto u*\Sigma,S)

P(\Pi,{\rm Arr}(t,t^{\prime})*\Sigma,S)

(Arr
\mapsto
)

\overset{{\rm def}}{=}

P(\Pi\land t^{\prime}>t,t\mapsto z*{\rm Arr}(t+1,t^{\prime})*\Sigma,S)

\land\ P(\Pi\land t^{\prime}=t,t\mapsto z^{\prime}*\Sigma,S)
,

where
(\Pi^{\prime\prime},t^{\prime\prime}\mapsto u^{\prime\prime}*\Sigma^{\prime\prime})\in S
and
z,z^{\prime}
are fresh

P(\Pi,{\rm Arr}(t,t^{\prime})*\Sigma,\{(\Pi_{i},{\rm Arr}(t_{i},t_{i}^{\prime})*\Sigma_{i})\}_{i\in I})

(ArrArr)

\overset{{\rm def}}{=}

\displaystyle\mathop{\bigwedge}_{I^{\prime}\subseteq I}P\left(\begin{array}[]{l}\Pi\land m=m_{I^{\prime}}\land m<m_{I\setminus I^{\prime}}\land t\leq t^{\prime}\land t^{\prime}<\Sigma\land 0<t,\Sigma,\\
\{(\Pi_{i}\land t=t_{i}\land t^{\prime}_{i}<\Sigma_{i},\Sigma_{i})\}_{i\in I^{\prime}}\cup\{(\Pi_{i}\land t=t_{i},{\rm Arr}(t_{i}+m+1,t_{i}^{\prime})*\Sigma_{i})\}_{i\in I\setminus I^{\prime}}\end{array}\right)

\land\displaystyle\mathop{\bigwedge}_{\emptyset\neq I^{\prime}\subseteq I}P\left(\begin{array}[]{l}\Pi\land m^{\prime}<m\land m^{\prime}=m_{I^{\prime}}\land m^{\prime}<m_{I\setminus I^{\prime}}\land 0<t,{\rm Arr}(t+m^{\prime}+1,t^{\prime})*\Sigma,\\
\{(\Pi_{i}\land t=t_{i}\land t^{\prime}_{i}<\Sigma_{i},\Sigma_{i})\}_{i\in I^{\prime}}\cup\{(\Pi_{i}\land t=t_{i},{\rm Arr}(t_{i}+m^{\prime}+1,t_{i}^{\prime})*\Sigma_{i})\}_{i\in I\setminus I^{\prime}}\end{array}\right)
,

where
m
,
m_{i}
, and
m^{\prime}
are abbreviations of
t^{\prime}-t
,
t_{i}^{\prime}-t_{i}
, and
m_{\min I^{\prime}}
, respectively.

Figure 1.
The translation
P

The formula
P(\Pi,\Sigma,\{(\Pi_{i},\Sigma_{i})\}_{i\in I})
means
that the QF entailment

\Pi\land\tilde{\Sigma}\vdash\{\Pi_{i}\land\tilde{\Sigma}\}_{i\in I}
is valid.
From this intuition, we sometimes call
\Sigma
the left spatial formula, and also call
\{\Sigma_{i}\}_{i\in I}
the right spatial formulas.
We call the left-most position of a spatial formula
the head position.
The atomic formula appears at the head position is called the head atom.

We will explain the meaning of each clause in Figure
1
.

The clauses
(EmpL)
and
(EmpR)
just remove
Emp

at the head position.

The clause
(EmpNEmp)
handles the case where
the left spatial formula is
Emp
.
A pair
(\Pi^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
in the third argument of
P
is removed
if
\Sigma^{\prime}
is not
Emp
,
since
\Pi^{\prime}\land\Sigma^{\prime}
cannot be satisfied by the empty heap.

The clause
(EmpEmp)
handles the case where
the left formula and all the right spatial formulas are
Emp
.
This case
P
returns a Presburger formula
which is equivalent to the corresponding entailment is valid.

The clause
(NEmpEmp)
handles the case where
the left spatial formula is not
Emp
and
a pair
(\Pi^{\prime},\hbox{Emp})
appears in the third argument of
P
.
We remove the pair since
\Pi^{\prime}\land\hbox{Emp}
cannot be satisfied
by any non-empty heap.
For example,

P(\hbox{True},x\mapsto 0*y\mapsto 0,\{(\hbox{True},\hbox{Emp})\})

becomes

P(\hbox{True},x\mapsto 0*y\mapsto 0,\emptyset)
.

The clause
(empty)
handles the case where
the third argument of
P
is empty.
This case
P
returns a Presburger formula which is equivalent to
that the left symbolic heap
\Pi\land\Sigma
is not satisfiable.
For example,

P(\hbox{True},x\mapsto 0*y\mapsto 0,\emptyset)

returns

\neg(x<y)
using the fact that
True
is the unit of
\land
.

The clause
(
\mapsto\mapsto
)
handles the case where
all the head atoms of
\Sigma
and
\{\Sigma_{i}\}_{i\in I}
are
the points-to atomic formulas.
This case we remove all of them and put equalities on the right pure parts.
By this rule the measure is strictly reduced.
For example,

P(\hbox{True},3\mapsto y*4\mapsto 11,\{(\hbox{True},x\mapsto y^{\prime}*{\rm Arr}(4,4))\})

becomes

P(3<4,4\mapsto 11,\{(3=x\land y=y^{\prime}\land x<4,{\rm Arr}(4,4))\})
.

The clause
(
\mapsto
Arr)
handles the case where
the head atom of the left spatial formula is the points-to atomic formula and
some right spatial formula
\Sigma_{i}
has the array atomic formula as its head atom.
Then we split the array atomic formula into a points-to atomic formula and an array atomic formula for the rest.
We have three subcases according to the size of the head array.
The first case is when the size of the array is
1
:
We replace the head array by a points-to atomic formula.
The second case is when the size of the head array is
greater than
1
:
We split the head array atomic formula into
a points-to atomic formula and an array atomic formula for the rest.
The last case is when the size of the head array is less than
1
:
We just remove
(\Pi_{i},\Sigma_{i})
, since the array atomic formula is false.
We note that this rule can be applied repeatedly until all head array atomic formulas of
the right spatial formulas are unfolded,
since the left spatial formula is unchanged.
Then the measure is eventually reduced by applying
(
\mapsto\mapsto
)
.
For example,

P(\hbox{True},x\mapsto 10,\{(\hbox{True},{\rm Arr}(4,5))\})

becomes

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle(5=4,x\mapsto 10,\{(\hbox{True},4\mapsto 10)\})

\displaystyle\land P(5>4,x\mapsto 10,\{(\hbox{True},4\mapsto 10*{\rm Arr}(5,5))\})

\displaystyle\land P(5<4,x\mapsto 10,\emptyset).

The clause
(Arr
\mapsto
)
handles the case where
the head atom of the left spatial formula is an array atomic formula and
there is a right spatial formula whose head atom is a points-to atomic formula.
We have two subcases according to the size of the head array.
The first case is the case where the size of the array is greater than
1
:
The array atomic formula is split into
a points-to atomic formula (with a fresh variable
z
)
and an array atomic formula for the rest.
The second case is when the size of the array is
1
:
The array atomic formula is unfolded and replaced by a points-to atomic formula with
a fresh variable
z^{\prime}
.
We note that the left head atom becomes a points-to atomic formula
after applying this rule.
Hence the measure is eventually reduced,
since
(
\mapsto\mapsto
)
or
(
\mapsto
Arr)
will be applied next.
For example,

P(\hbox{True},{\rm Arr}(x,3),\{(\hbox{True},y\mapsto 10)\})

becomes

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle(x<3,x\mapsto z*{\rm Arr}(x+1,3),\{(\hbox{True},y\mapsto 10)\})

\displaystyle\land P(x=3,x\mapsto z^{\prime},\{(\hbox{True},y\mapsto 10)\}).

The last clause
(ArrArr)
handles the case where
all the head atoms in the left and right spatial formulas are array atomic formulas.
We first find the head arrays of the shortest length among the head arrays.
Next we split each longer array into two arrays
so that the first part has the same size as the shortest array.
Then we remove the first parts. The shortest arrays are also removed.
In this operation we have two subcases:
The first case is when the array atomic formula of the left spatial formula
has the shortest size and disappears by the operation.
The second case is when the array atomic formula of the left spatial formula
has a longer size, it is split into two array atomic formulas, and the second part remains.
We note that the measure is strictly reduced,
since at least one shortest array atomic formula is removed.
For example,

P(\hbox{True},{\rm Arr}(x,5),\{(\hbox{True},{\rm Arr}(y,2)*{\rm Arr}(3,z))\})

becomes

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle(5-x=2-y\land x\leq 5\land 0<x,\hbox{Emp},\{(x=y\land 2<3,{\rm Arr}(3,z))\})

\displaystyle\land P(5-x<2-y\land x\leq 5\land 0<x,\hbox{Emp},

\displaystyle\qquad\{(x=y,{\rm Arr}(y+(5-x)+1,2)*{\rm Arr}(3,z))\})

\displaystyle\land P(2-y<5-x\land 0<x,{\rm Arr}(x+(2-y)+1,5),\{(x=y\land 2<3,{\rm Arr}(3,z))\}).

This example is a case when
I
is a singleton set (we write
\{0\}
for it),

\Pi
and
\Pi_{0}
are
True
,
\Sigma
is
Emp
,
\Sigma_{0}
is
{\rm Arr}(3,z)
,

t
is
x
,
t^{\prime}
is
5
,
t_{0}
is
y
,
t^{\prime}_{0}
is
2
in
(ArrArr)
.
The first clause of this result is obtained from the case
I^{\prime}=I
of the first conjunct in
(ArrArr)
,
namely,
m=m_{I^{\prime}}
is
5-x=2-y
and
m<m_{I\setminus I^{\prime}}
is
True
.
The second clause is obtained from the case
I^{\prime}=\emptyset
of the first conjunct,
namely,
m=m_{I^{\prime}}
is
True
and
m<m_{I\setminus I^{\prime}}
is
5-x<2-y
.
The last clause is obtained from the case
I^{\prime}=I
of the second conjunct in
(ArrArr)
,
namely,
m^{\prime}<m
is
2-y<5-x
,
m^{\prime}=m_{I^{\prime}}
is
2-y=5-x
,
and
m^{\prime}<m_{I\setminus I^{\prime}}
is
True
.

{exa}

The sorted entailment

(x\mapsto 10*v\mapsto 11)^{\sim}\vdash{\rm Arr}(x^{\prime},v^{\prime})^{\sim}

is translated by computing

P(\hbox{True},x\mapsto 10*v\mapsto 11,\{(\hbox{True},{\rm Arr}(x^{\prime},v^{\prime}))\})
.
We will see its calculation step by step.
It first becomes

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle(x^{\prime}=v^{\prime},x\mapsto 10*v\mapsto 11,\{(\hbox{True},x^{\prime}\mapsto 10)\})

\Pi
to be
x^{\prime}<v^{\prime}\land x<v

3.3.
Decidability Theorem

The aim of
P
is to give an equivalent formula of Presburger arithmetic to a given entailment.
The correctness property of
P
is stated as follows.

Proposition 2

(Correctness of Translation
P
)
.

If any array atomic formula in
\Sigma_{i}
has the form
{\rm Arr}(t,t+u)

such that the term
u
does not contain
\overrightarrow{y}
, then

\begin{array}[]{l}\Pi\land\tilde{\Sigma}\models\{\exists\overrightarrow{y_{i}}(\Pi_{i}\land\tilde{\Sigma}_{i})\}_{i\in I}\quad\hbox{ iff }\quad\models\forall\overrightarrow{z}\exists\overrightarrow{y}P(\Pi,\Sigma,\{(\Pi_{i},\Sigma_{i})\}_{i\in I})\end{array}

where
\overrightarrow{y}
is a sequence of
\overrightarrow{y_{i}}
(
i\in I
), and

\overrightarrow{z}
is
\hbox{FV}(P(\Pi,\Sigma,\{(\Pi_{i},\Sigma_{i})\}_{i\in I}))\setminus\hbox{FV}(\Pi,\Sigma,\{\Pi_{i}\}_{i\in I},\{\Sigma_{i}\}_{i\in I})
.

We note that
\overrightarrow{z}
are the fresh variables introduced in the unfolding of
P(\Pi,\Sigma,\{(\Pi_{i},\Sigma_{i})\}_{i\in I})
.
The proof of this theorem will be given in the next section.

The correctness property is shown with the condition described in the theorem.
This condition avoids a complicated situation for
\overrightarrow{y}
and
\overrightarrow{z}
,
such that some variables in
\overrightarrow{y}
depend on
\overrightarrow{z}
, and some determine
\overrightarrow{z}
.
For example, if we consider
{\rm Arr}(1,5)\vdash\exists y_{1}y_{2}({\rm Arr}(1,y_{1})*y_{1}+1\mapsto y_{2}*{\rm Arr}(y_{1}+2,5))
,
we will have
y_{1}+1\mapsto z
during the unfolding of

P(\hbox{True},{\rm Arr}(1,5),\{(\hbox{True},{\rm Arr}(1,y_{1})*y_{1}+1\mapsto y_{2}*{\rm Arr}(y_{1}+2,5))\})
.
Then we have
z=y_{2}
after some logical simplification.
This fact means that
y_{2}
depends on
z
,
and moreover
z
is indirectly determined by
y_{1}
.
The latter case occurs when sizes of array depend on
\overrightarrow{y}
.
We need to exclude this situation.

Finally we have the decidability result for the entailment problem of
SLA
under the condition from the above theorem and the property of sorted entailments (stated in Lemma
1
).

Theorem 3

(Decidability of Validity Checking of Entailments in
SLA
)
.

Validity checking of entailments

\Pi\land\Sigma\vdash\{\exists\overrightarrow{y_{i}}(\Pi_{i}\land\Sigma_{i})\}_{i\in I}
in
SLA

is decidable,
if any array atomic formula in
\Sigma_{i}
has the form
{\rm Arr}(t,t+u)
such that the term
u
does not contain
\overrightarrow{y_{i}}
.

{exa}

An example

{\rm Arr}(x,x)\vdash x\mapsto 0,\exists y(y>0\land x\mapsto y)

satisfies the condition, and its validity is checked in the following way.

•

It is decomposed into several sorted entailments: in this case, it produces one sorted entailment

{\rm Arr}(x,x)^{\sim}\vdash x\mapsto 0,\exists y(y>0\land x\mapsto y)

•

Compute
P(\hbox{True},{\rm Arr}(x,x),S_{1})
,
where
S_{1}
is
\{(\hbox{True},x\mapsto 0),(y>0,x\mapsto y)\}
.
It becomes

P(x<x,x\mapsto z*{\rm Arr}(x+1,x),S_{1})\land P(x=x,x\mapsto z,S_{1})

by (Arr
\mapsto
). Then it becomes

\begin{array}[]{l}P(x<x\land x<x+1,{\rm Arr}(x+1,x),S_{2})\land P(x=x\land x<x+1,\hbox{Emp},S_{2}),\end{array}

where
S_{2}
is

\{(x=x\land z=0,\hbox{Emp}),(y>0\land x=x\land z=y,\hbox{Emp})\}
.
The former conjunct becomes
P(x<x\land x<x+1,{\rm Arr}(x+1,x),\emptyset)
by (NEmpEmp),
then, by (empty), it becomes

\neg(x<x\land x<x+1\land x+1\leq x)
.
The latter conjunct becomes

x=x\land x<x+1\to(x=x\land z=0)\vee(y>0\land x=x\land z=y)
,
which is equivalent to
x<x+1\to z=0\vee(y>0\land z=y)
,

•

Check the validity of the formula

\forall xz\exists yP(\hbox{True},{\rm Arr}(x,x),S_{1})
,
which is equivalent to

\forall xz\exists y(\neg(x<x\land x<x+1\land x+1\leq x)\land(z=0\vee(y>0\land z=y)))
.
Finally the procedure answers “valid”, since the produced Presburger formula is valid.

3.4.
Other Systems of Symbolic Heaps with Arrays

Other known systems of symbolic heaps with arrays are only
the system given in Brotherston et al.
[
7
]
.
They gave a different condition for decidability of the entailment problem for the same symbolic-heap system.
Their condition disallows existential variables in
u

for each points-to atomic formula
t\mapsto u
in the succedent of an entailment.
In order to clarify the difference between our condition and their condition,
we consider the following entailments:

1.

{\rm Arr}(x,x)\vdash x\mapsto 0,\exists y(y>0\land x\mapsto y)
,

2.

{\rm Arr}(1,5)\vdash\exists y,y^{\prime}({\rm Arr}(y,y+1)*{\rm Arr}(y^{\prime},y^{\prime}+2))
,

3.

{\rm Arr}(1,5)\vdash\exists y({\rm Arr}(1,1+y)*{\rm Arr}(2+y,5))
,

4.

{\rm Arr}(1,5)\vdash\exists y,y^{\prime}({\rm Arr}(1,1+y)*2+y\mapsto y^{\prime}*{\rm Arr}(3+y,5))
.

The entailment (i) can be decided by our decision procedure, but it cannot be decided by their procedure.
The entailment (ii) is decided by both theirs and ours.
The entailment (iii) is decided by theirs, but it does not satisfy our condition.
The entailment (iv) is decided by neither theirs nor ours.

Our system and the system in
[
7
]
have the
same purpose, namely, analysis/verification of memory safety.
Basically their target programming language and assertion language are
the same as ours given in this section.
These entailment checkers are essentially used for deciding the side condition
of the consequence rule.
As explained above, ours and theirs have different restrictions for decidability.
Hence the class of programs is the same for
ours and theirs, but some triples can be proved only by ours and
other triples can be proved only by theirs,
according to the shape of assertions.
In this sense both our system and their system
have advantage and disadvantage.

4.
Correctness of Decision Procedure

This section shows correctness of our decision procedure.
We first show the basic property of sorted entailments.

4.1.
Correctness of Translation

This subsection shows correctness of the translation
P
.
The main difficulty for showing correctness is how to handle the new variables (denoted by
z
)
that are introduced during the unfolding
P
.
In order to do this, we temporarily extend our language with new terms, denoted by
[t]
.
A term
[t]
means the value at the address
t
, that is,
it is interpreted to
h(s(t))
under
(s,h)
.
We will use this notation instead of
z
, since
z
must appear in the form
t\mapsto z
during unfolding
P
, and this
t
is unique for
z
.
Note that both
s
and
h
are necessary for interpreting
a formula of the extended language even
if it is a pure formula.

In this extended language, we temporarily introduce a variant
P^{\prime}
of
P

so that we use
[t]
instead of
z
,
which is defined in the same way as
P
except

\displaystyle P^{\prime}(\Pi,{\rm Arr}(t,t^{\prime})*\Sigma,S)\overset{{\rm def}}{=}

\displaystyle P^{\prime}(\Pi\land t^{\prime}=t,t\mapsto[t]*\Sigma,S)

\displaystyle\land P^{\prime}(\Pi\land t^{\prime}>t,t\mapsto[t]*{\rm Arr}(t+1,t^{\prime})*\Sigma,S),

when
(\Pi^{\prime\prime},t^{\prime\prime}\mapsto u^{\prime\prime}*\Sigma^{\prime\prime})\in S
.
Note that
P^{\prime}
never introduces any new variables.

We will introduce some notations.
Let
S
be
\{(\Pi,\Sigma)\}_{i\in I}
.
Then we write
\tilde{S}
for
\{\Pi_{i}\land\tilde{\Sigma_{i}}\}_{i\in I}
.
We write
{\rm Dom}(s,\Sigma)
for
the set of addresses used by
\Sigma
under
s
, that is,
it is inductively defined as follows:

{\rm Dom}(s,\hbox{Emp})=\emptyset
,

{\rm Dom}(s,\hbox{Emp}*\Sigma_{1})={\rm Dom}(s,\Sigma_{1})
,

{\rm Dom}(s,t\mapsto u*\Sigma_{1})=\{s(t)\}\cup{\rm Dom}(s,\Sigma_{1})
, and

{\rm Dom}(s,{\rm Arr}(t,u)*\Sigma_{1})=\{s(t),\ldots,s(u)\}\cup{\rm Dom}(s,\Sigma_{1})
if
s(t)\leq s(u)
.

The next lemma clarifies the connections between entailments,
P
, and
P^{\prime}
.

Lemma 4
.

(1)

Assume
s,h\models\hat{\Pi}\land\hat{\Sigma}
.
Suppose
P^{\prime}(\Pi,\Sigma,S)
appears in the unfolding of
P^{\prime}(\hat{\Pi},\hat{\Sigma},\hat{S})
.
Then

s,h|_{{\rm Dom}(s,\Sigma)}\models P^{\prime}(\Pi,\Sigma,S)

iff

s,h|_{{\rm Dom}(s,\Sigma)}\models\Pi\land{\rm Sorted}(\Sigma)\mathbin{\to}\displaystyle\mathop{\bigvee}\tilde{S}.

(2)

\forall sh(s,h\models\Pi\land\tilde{\Sigma}\mathbin{\to}s,h\models\exists\overrightarrow{y}P^{\prime}(\Pi,\Sigma,S))

iff

\Pi\land\tilde{\Sigma}\models\exists\overrightarrow{y}\displaystyle\mathop{\bigvee}\tilde{S}.

(3)

\models\neg(\Pi\land{\rm Sorted}(\Sigma))\mathbin{\to}P(\Pi,\Sigma,S)
.

(4)

Let
\overrightarrow{z}
be fresh variables introduced during the calculation of
P(\Pi,\Sigma,S)
.
Then

\forall sh(s,h\models\Pi\land\tilde{\Sigma}\mathbin{\to}s,h\models\forall\overrightarrow{z}\exists\overrightarrow{y}P(\Pi,\Sigma,S))

iff

\models\forall\overrightarrow{z}\exists\overrightarrow{y}P(\Pi,\Sigma,S).

Proof of Lemma
4
(1)
.  This is shown by induction on the steps
\overset{{\rm def}}{=}
.
Consider cases according to the definition of
P^{\prime}
.

Case 1
(
\mapsto\mapsto
-case):

\displaystyle P^{\prime}

\displaystyle(\Pi,t\mapsto u*\Sigma,\{(\Pi_{i},t_{i}\mapsto u_{i}*\Sigma_{i})\}_{i\in I})

\displaystyle\overset{{\rm def}}{=}P^{\prime}(\Pi\land t<\Sigma,\Sigma,\{(\Pi_{i}\land t=t_{i}\land u=u_{i}\land t_{i}<\Sigma_{i},\Sigma_{i})\}_{i\in I}).

Let
h_{1}=h|_{{\rm Dom}(s,t\mapsto u*\Sigma)}
,

.
Then
h_{1}=\{(s(t),h(s(t)))\}+h_{2}
.
It is enough to show

(a)

iff

s,h_{2}\models\Pi\land t<\Sigma\land{\rm Sorted}(\Sigma)\mathbin{\to}\displaystyle\mathop{\bigvee}_{i\in I}\Pi_{i}\land t=t_{i}\land u=u_{i}\land t_{i}<\Sigma_{i}\land\Sigma_{i}^{\sim}.

(b)

The only-if part.
Assume (
a
) and
the antecedent of (

).
Then the antecedent of (

) holds, since it is equivalent to the antecedent of (

Then the succedent of (

) is true for

Hence the succedent of (

) and

Then the antecedent of (

).
Then the succedent of (

Hence the succedent of (

(

\displaystyle P^{\prime}(\Pi,{\rm Arr}(t,t^{\prime})*\Sigma,S)\overset{{\rm def}}{=}

\displaystyle\land P^{\prime}(\Pi\land t^{\prime}>t,t\mapsto[t]*{\rm Arr}(t+1,t^{\prime})*\Sigma,S).

Let

(c)

is equivalent to the conjunction of the following two clauses:

(d)

and

\displaystyle P^{\prime}(\Pi,

\displaystyle\land P^{\prime}(\Pi\land t_{i}^{\prime}>t_{i},t\mapsto u*\Sigma,\{(\Pi_{i},t_{i}\mapsto u*{\rm Arr}(t_{i}+1,t_{i}^{\prime})*\Sigma_{i})\}\cup S)

\displaystyle\models\Pi\land t_{i}^{\prime}=t_{i}\land{\rm Sorted}_{L}\mathbin{\to}\Pi_{i}\land(t_{i}\mapsto u*\Sigma_{i})^{\sim}\lor\displaystyle\mathop{\bigvee}\tilde{S}

\displaystyle s,h^{\prime}

\displaystyle\models\Pi\land t_{i}^{\prime}<t_{i}\land{\rm Sorted}_{L}\mathbin{\to}\displaystyle\mathop{\bigvee}\tilde{S}.

(

such that

Case 4.2:
the case of

.

Proof of Lemma
4
(2)
. We first show the only-if part.
Assume the left-hand side of the claim and

s,h\models\Pi\land\tilde{\Sigma}
.
By the left-hand side, we obtain

s,h\models\exists\overrightarrow{y}P^{\prime}(\Pi,\Sigma,S)
.
Hence we have
s^{\prime}
such that
s^{\prime},h\models P^{\prime}(\Pi,\Sigma,S)
.
By (1),

s^{\prime},h\models\Pi\land{\rm Sorted}(\Sigma)\mathbin{\to}\displaystyle\mathop{\bigvee}S
.
Thus
s^{\prime},h\models\displaystyle\mathop{\bigvee}S
.
Finally we have
s,h\models\exists\overrightarrow{y}\displaystyle\mathop{\bigvee}S
.

Next we show the if part.
Fix
s,h
.
Assume the right-hand side of the claim and

s,h\models\Pi\land\tilde{\Sigma}
.
By the right-hand side,
s,h\models\exists\overrightarrow{y}\displaystyle\mathop{\bigvee}\tilde{S}
holds.
Hence we have
s^{\prime}
such that
s^{\prime},h\models\displaystyle\mathop{\bigvee}S
.
Then we obtain
s^{\prime},h\models\Pi\land{\rm Sorted}(\Sigma)\mathbin{\to}\displaystyle\mathop{\bigvee}\tilde{S}
.
By (1),
s^{\prime},h\models P^{\prime}(\Pi,\Sigma,S)
holds.
Finally we have
s,h\models\exists\overrightarrow{y}P^{\prime}(\Pi,\Sigma,S)
.

Proof of Lemma
4
(3)
. It is shown by induction on the steps of
\overset{{\rm def}}{=}
.
Consider cases according to the definition of
P
.

Case 1
((
EmpL
)-case):
By the induction hypothesis we have

\models\neg(\Pi\land{\rm Sorted}(\Sigma))\mathbin{\to}P(\Pi,\Sigma,S)
.
This is equivalent to

\models\neg(\Pi\land{\rm Sorted}(\hbox{Emp}*\Sigma))\mathbin{\to}P(\Pi,\hbox{Emp}*\Sigma,S)
.

The other cases (
EmpR
), (
EmpNEmp
), (
NEmpEmp
), and (
\mapsto\mapsto
)
are shown in a similar way. We will consider the remaining cases.

Case 2
((
EmpEmp
)-case):
This case is easily shown, since

\models\neg(\Pi\land{\rm Sorted}(\hbox{Emp}))\mathbin{\to}(\Pi\mathbin{\to}\bigvee_{i\in I}\Pi_{i})
trivially holds.

Case 3
((
empty
)-case):
This case is easily shown, since

\models\neg(\Pi\land{\rm Sorted}(\hbox{Emp}))\mathbin{\to}\neg(\Pi\land{\rm Sorted}(\hbox{Emp}))
trivially holds.

Case 4
((
\mapsto
Arr
)-case):
By the induction hypothesis, we have

\begin{array}[]{l}\models\neg(\Pi\land t^{\prime}_{i}=t_{i}\land{\rm Sorted}(t\mapsto u*\Sigma))\mathbin{\to}P^{(=)},\end{array}

where
P^{(=)}
is an abbreviation of
P(\Pi\land t^{\prime}_{i}=t_{i},t\mapsto u*\Sigma,\{(\Pi_{i},t_{i}\mapsto u*\Sigma_{i})\}\cup S)
,

\begin{array}[]{l}\models\neg(\Pi\land t^{\prime}_{i}>t_{i}\land{\rm Sorted}(t\mapsto u*\Sigma))\mathbin{\to}P^{(>)},\end{array}

where

P^{(>)}
is an abbreviation of
P(\Pi\land t^{\prime}_{i}>t_{i},t\mapsto u*\Sigma,\{(\Pi_{i},t_{i}\mapsto u*{\rm Arr}(t_{i}+1,t_{i}^{\prime})*\Sigma_{i})\}\cup S)
, and

\begin{array}[]{l}\models\neg(\Pi\land t^{\prime}_{i}<t_{i}\land{\rm Sorted}(t\mapsto u*\Sigma))\mathbin{\to}P^{(<)},\end{array}

where
P^{(<)}
is an abbreviation of
P(\Pi\land t^{\prime}_{i}<t_{i},t\mapsto u*\Sigma,S)
.
In order to show the current case, it is enough to show

\Pi\land t^{\prime}_{i}=t_{i}\land{\rm Sorted}(t\mapsto u*\Sigma)\mathbin{\to}\Pi\land{\rm Sorted}(t\mapsto u*\Sigma)
,

\Pi\land t^{\prime}_{i}>t_{i}\land{\rm Sorted}(t\mapsto u*\Sigma)\mathbin{\to}\Pi\land{\rm Sorted}(t\mapsto u*\Sigma)
, and

\Pi\land t^{\prime}_{i}<t_{i}\land{\rm Sorted}(t\mapsto u*\Sigma)\mathbin{\to}\Pi\land{\rm Sorted}(t\mapsto u*\Sigma)
.
They trivially hold by comparing conjuncts.

Case 5
((
Arr
\mapsto
)-case):
By the induction hypothesis, we have

\begin{array}[]{l}\models\neg(\Pi\land t^{\prime}>t\land{\rm Sorted}(t\mapsto z*{\rm Arr}(t+1,t^{\prime})*\Sigma))\mathbin{\to}P^{(>)},\end{array}

where
P^{(>)}
is an abbreviation of
P(\Pi\land t^{\prime}>t,t\mapsto z*{\rm Arr}(t+1,t^{\prime})*\Sigma,S)
, and

\begin{array}[]{l}\models\neg(\Pi\land t^{\prime}=t\land{\rm Sorted}(t\mapsto z^{\prime}*\Sigma))\mathbin{\to}P^{(=)},\end{array}

where
P^{(=)}
is an abbreviation of
P(\Pi\land t^{\prime}=t,t\mapsto z^{\prime}*\Sigma,S)
.
In order to show the current case, it is enough to show

\Pi\land t^{\prime}>t\land{\rm Sorted}(t\mapsto z*{\rm Arr}(t+1,t^{\prime})*\Sigma)\mathbin{\to}\Pi\land{\rm Sorted}({\rm Arr}(t,t^{\prime})*\Sigma)

and

\Pi\land t^{\prime}=t\land{\rm Sorted}(t\mapsto z^{\prime}*\Sigma)\mathbin{\to}\Pi\land{\rm Sorted}({\rm Arr}(t,t^{\prime})*\Sigma)
.
They are immediately shown by the definition of
{\rm Sorted}
.

Case 6
((
ArrArr
)-case):
For
I^{\prime}\subseteq I
, we abbreviate

m=m_{I^{\prime}}\land m<m_{I\setminus I^{\prime}}

and

m^{\prime}<m\land m^{\prime}=m_{I^{\prime}}\land m^{\prime}<m_{I\setminus I^{\prime}}
by

\Pi^{(=)}_{I^{\prime}}
and
\Pi^{(<)}_{I^{\prime}}
, respectively.
We write
S^{(=)}_{I^{\prime}}
and
S^{(<)}_{I^{\prime}}
for the third arguments of the first and the second
P
for
I^{\prime}

in the right-hand side of (ArrArr), respectively.
We also write
{\rm Arr}
for
{\rm Arr}(t+m^{\prime}+1,t^{\prime})
.
By the induction hypothesis, we obtain the forms of

\displaystyle\models\neg(\Pi\land\Pi^{(=)}_{I^{\prime}}\land t\leq t^{\prime}\land

\displaystyle t^{\prime}<\Sigma\land 0<t\land{\rm Sorted}(\Sigma))

\displaystyle\mathbin{\to}P(\Pi\land\Pi^{(=)}_{I^{\prime}}\land t\leq t^{\prime}\land t^{\prime}<\Sigma\land 0<t,\Sigma,S^{(=)}_{I^{\prime}})

for each
I^{\prime}\subseteq I
, and

\displaystyle\models\neg(\Pi\land\Pi^{(<)}_{I^{\prime}}\land 0<t\land{\rm Sorted}({\rm Arr}*\Sigma))\mathbin{\to}P(\Pi\land\Pi^{(<)}_{I^{\prime}}\land 0<t,{\rm Arr}*\Sigma,S^{(<)}_{I^{\prime}})

for each
I^{\prime}\subseteq I
with
I^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
.
In order to show the current case, it is enough to show

\Pi\land\Pi^{(=)}_{I^{\prime}}\land t\leq t^{\prime}\land t^{\prime}<\Sigma\land 0<t\land{\rm Sorted}(\Sigma)\mathbin{\to}\Pi\land{\rm Sorted}({\rm Arr}(t,t^{\prime})*\Sigma)
for each
I^{\prime}\subseteq I
,
and

\Pi\land\Pi^{(<)}_{I^{\prime}}\land 0<t\land{\rm Sorted}({\rm Arr}*\Sigma)\mathbin{\to}\Pi\land{\rm Sorted}({\rm Arr}(t,t^{\prime})*\Sigma)

for each
I^{\prime}\subseteq I
with
I^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
.
They are immediately shown by the definition of
{\rm Sorted}
.

Proof of Lemma
4
(4)
. We note that

\forall sh(s,h\models\Pi\land\tilde{\Sigma}\mathbin{\to}s\models\forall\overrightarrow{z}\exists\overrightarrow{y}P(\Pi,\Sigma,S))

is equivalent to

\forall s(\exists h(s,h\models\Pi\land\tilde{\Sigma})\mathbin{\to}s\models\forall\overrightarrow{z}\exists\overrightarrow{y}P(\Pi,\Sigma,S))
.
Moreover it is equivalent to

\forall s(s\models\Pi\land{\rm Sorted}(\Sigma)\mathbin{\to}s\models\forall\overrightarrow{z}\exists\overrightarrow{y}P(\Pi,\Sigma,S))
.
By (3), we have

\models\neg(\Pi\land{\rm Sorted}(\Sigma))\mathbin{\to}\forall\overrightarrow{z}\exists\overrightarrow{y}P(\Pi,\Sigma,S)
, since

\Pi\land{\rm Sorted}(\Sigma)
does not contain
\overrightarrow{z}
,
which are fresh variables
introduced during the calculation of
P(\Pi,\Sigma,S)
.
Hence we obtain

\models(\Pi\land{\rm Sorted}(\Sigma))\vee\neg(\Pi\land{\rm Sorted}(\Sigma))\mathbin{\to}\forall\overrightarrow{z}\exists\overrightarrow{y}P(\Pi,\Sigma,S)
.
This is equivalent to

\models\forall\overrightarrow{z}\exists\overrightarrow{y}P(\Pi,\Sigma,S)
.
∎

4.2.
Decidability Proof

This subsection proves the correctness of the decision procedure.

Proof of Proposition
2

Let
S
be
\{(\Pi_{i},\Sigma_{i})\}_{i\in I}
.
Then the left-hand side is equivalent to

\Pi\land\tilde{\Sigma}\models\exists\overrightarrow{y}\displaystyle\mathop{\bigvee}\tilde{S}
.
Moreover, by Lemma
4
(2), it is equivalent to

\forall sh(s,h\models\Pi\land\tilde{\Sigma}\mathbin{\to}s,h\models\exists\overrightarrow{y}P^{\prime}(\Pi,\Sigma,S)).

(i)

By Lemma
4
(4), the right-hand side is equivalent to

\forall sh(s,h\models\Pi\land\tilde{\Sigma}\mathbin{\to}s\models\forall\overrightarrow{z}\exists\overrightarrow{y}P(\Pi,\Sigma,S)).

(j)

Now we will show the equivalence of (
i
) and (
j
).
Here we assume
[t_{1}],\ldots,[t_{n}]
appear in
P^{\prime}(\Pi,\Sigma,S)

and
s\models t_{1}<\ldots<t_{n}
,
we let
\overrightarrow{z}=z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}
.

Recall that our condition requires that
sizes of arrays in the succedent do not depend on existential variables.
We note that, under the condition of Theorem
3
,
each
t
of
t\mapsto u
or
{\rm Arr}(t,t^{\prime})
in the second argument of
P^{\prime}
during the unfolding of
P^{\prime}
does not contain any existential variables.
By this fact, we can see that each term
[t]
does not contain existential variables,
since it first appears as
t\mapsto[t]
in the second argument of
P^{\prime}
during the unfolding of
P^{\prime}
.
So we can obtain
P^{\prime}(\Pi,\Sigma,S)=P(\Pi,\Sigma,S)[\overrightarrow{z}:=[\overrightarrow{t}]]
.
Hence (
i
) is obtained from (
j
) by taking
z_{i}
to be
[t_{i}]
for
1\leq i\leq n
.

We show the inverse direction. Assume (
i
).
Fix
s
and
h
such that
s,h\models\Pi\land\tilde{\Sigma}
.
We will show

s\models\forall\overrightarrow{z}\exists\overrightarrow{y}P(\Pi,\Sigma,S)
.
Take
\overrightarrow{a}
for
\overrightarrow{z}
.
Let
s^{\prime}
be
s[\overrightarrow{z}:=\overrightarrow{a}]
.
We claim that
s(t_{j})\in{\rm Dom}(h)
(
j=1,\ldots,n
),
since each
t_{j}
appears as an address of an array atomic formula in
\Sigma
.
Define
h^{\prime}
by
{\rm Dom}(h^{\prime})={\rm Dom}(h)
and
h^{\prime}(m)=a_{j}
if
m=s(t_{j})
(
j=1,\ldots,n
), otherwise
h^{\prime}(m)=h(m)
.
Then we have
s,h^{\prime}\models\Pi\land\tilde{\Sigma}
.
By (
i
), we obtain
s,h^{\prime}\models\exists\overrightarrow{y}P^{\prime}(\Pi,\Sigma,S)
.
Hence
s^{\prime}\models\exists\overrightarrow{y}P(\Pi,\Sigma,S)
holds,
since
s^{\prime},h^{\prime}\models[\overrightarrow{t}]=\overrightarrow{z}
.
Therefore we have
s\models\forall\overrightarrow{z}\exists\overrightarrow{y}P(\Pi,\Sigma,S)
.
Thus we obtain (
j
).
∎

Hence we obtained the decidability result of
SLA
stated in Theorem
3
.

5.
Separation Logic with Arrays and Lists

From this section,
we will show the second result of this paper:
the decidability of validity checking for QF entailments of symbolic heap system with array and list predicates.
The decidability result of the previous section will be used in that of the second result.

We start from the separation logic
{\bf G}^{+}
, which is obtained from
G

by assuming the point-to predicate is ternary
and
adding the singly-linked list predicate
{\rm ls}(\hbox{-},\hbox{-})

and the doubly-linked list predicate
{\rm dll}(\hbox{-},\hbox{-},\hbox{-},\hbox{-})
.
Then we define the symbolic heap system
SLAL
which is a fragment of
{\bf G}^{+}
.

5.1.
Syntax of
{\bf G}^{+}
and
SLAL

The terms of
{\bf G}^{+}
are same as those of
G
.
The formulas (denoted by
F
) of
{\bf G}^{+}
are defined as follows:

F::=t=t\ |\ F\land F\ |\ \neg F\ |\ \exists xF\ |\ \hbox{Emp}\ |\ F*F\ |\ t\mapsto(t,t)\ |\ {\rm Arr}(t,t)\ |\ {\rm ls}(t,t)\ |\ {\rm dll}(t,t,t,t)
.

The notations for
G
mentioned in Section
2
are also used for
{\bf G}^{+}
.

We call the singly-linked and doubly-linked list predicates
list predicates
.
We also call a formula
list-free
if it does not contain any list predicates.

The list predicates are inductively defined predicates and they are introduced with the following definitions clauses:

\displaystyle{\rm ls}(x,y)

\displaystyle::=(x=y\land\hbox{Emp})\vee\exists zw(x\mapsto(z,w)*{\rm ls}(z,y)),

\displaystyle{\rm dll}(x_{1},y_{1},x_{2},y_{2})

\displaystyle::=(x_{1}=y_{1}\land x_{2}=y_{2}\land\hbox{Emp})\vee\exists x(x_{1}\mapsto(x,y_{2})*{\rm dll}(x,y_{1},x_{2},x_{1})).

We define the
k
-times unfolding of the list predicates.

{defi}

For
k\geq 0
,
{\rm ls}^{k}(t,u)
and
{\rm dll}^{k}(t,u,v,w)
are inductively defined by:

{\rm ls}^{0}(t,u)\overset{{\rm def}}{=}0\neq 0\land\hbox{Emp}
,

{\rm ls}^{k+1}(t,u)\overset{{\rm def}}{=}(t=u\land\hbox{Emp})\vee\exists zw(t\mapsto(z,w)*{\rm ls}^{k}(z,u))
,

{\rm dll}^{0}(t,u,v,w)\overset{{\rm def}}{=}0\neq 0\land\hbox{Emp}
, and

{\rm dll}^{k+1}(t,u,v,w)\overset{{\rm def}}{=}(t=u\land v=w\land\hbox{Emp})\vee\exists z(t\mapsto(z,w)*{\rm dll}^{k}(z,u,v,t))
.

For each formula
F
of
{\bf G}^{+}
and heap model
(s,h)
, we define
s,h\models F
extending that of
G
with

\displaystyle s,h

\displaystyle\models{\rm ls}(t,u)\overset{{\rm def}}{\Longleftrightarrow}s,h\models{\rm ls}^{k}(t,u)\hbox{ for some $k\geq 0$, and}

\displaystyle s,h

\displaystyle\models{\rm dll}(t,u,v,w)\overset{{\rm def}}{\Longleftrightarrow}s,h\models{\rm dll}^{k}(t,u,v,w)\hbox{ for some $k\geq 0$.}

We note that there is no heap model
(s,h)
such that
s,h\models{\rm ls}^{0}(t,u)
.
Intuitively a heap model
(s,h)
that satisfies
s,h\models{\rm ls}^{k+1}(t,u)
contains a singly-linked list
of length
k
starting from
s(t)
and ends at
s(u)
.
This situation is depicted as follows:

\begin{array}[]{l}\hbox to156.89pt{\vbox to30.25pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-2.64545pt\lower-18.09901pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

We also note that there is no heap model
(s,h)
such that
s,h\models{\rm dll}^{0}(t,u,v,w)
.
A heap model
(s,h)
that satisfies
s,h\models{\rm dll}^{k+1}(t,u,v,w)
contains
a doubly-linked list of length
k

with a forward-link starting from
s(t)
and ending at
s(u)
, and
a backward-link starting from
s(v)
and ending at
s(w)
.
This situation is depicted as follows:

The notation
F\models\overrightarrow{F}
is also defined in a similar way to
SLA
.

Formulas of
SLAL
are QF symbolic heaps (denoted by
\varphi
) of the form
\Pi\land\Sigma
,
where its pure part
\Pi
is the same as that of
SLA
and its spatial part
\Sigma
is defined by

\begin{array}[]{l}\Sigma::=\hbox{Emp}\mid t\mapsto(t,t)\mid{\rm Arr}(t,t)\mid{\rm ls}(t,t)\mid{\rm dll}(t,t,t,t)\mid\Sigma*\Sigma.\end{array}

We use notations
\Pi_{\varphi}
and
\Sigma_{\varphi}
that mean the pure part
and the spatial part of
\varphi
, respectively.

Entailments of
SLAL
are QF entailments of the form

\varphi\vdash\{\varphi_{i}\ |\ i\in I\}
.
The validity of an entailment is defined in a similar way to
SLA
.

6.
Unroll Collapse

This section shows the unroll collapse properties
for the singly-linked and doubly-linked list predicates.
In the decision procedure for
SLAL
, these properties will be used
for eliminating the list predicates in the antecedent of a given entailment.

The unroll collapse property for the singly-linked list predicate is given in the next proposition.
The key idea of its proof is to replace the list
{\rm ls}(t,u)
by some list

{\rm ls}(t,u)
of length 2 in the antecedent of (1), in order to show (1).
Then (2) applies to obtain the succedent. Then we can show that the
list of length 2 is contained in some list
{\rm ls}(p,q)
of the
succedent. By replacing the list of length 2 by
{\rm ls}(t,u)
of arbitrary
length, we obtain the succedent of (1), since the difference by the
replacement is absorbed by
{\rm ls}(p,q)
.

Proposition 5

(Unroll Collapse for
{\rm ls}
)
.

The following clauses are equivalent:

(1)
{\rm ls}(t,u)*\phi\models\overrightarrow{\psi}
,

(2)
t=u\land\phi\models\overrightarrow{\psi}

and

t\mapsto(z,y_{1})*z\mapsto(u,y_{2})*\phi\models\overrightarrow{\psi}
,
where
z,y_{1},y_{2}
are fresh.

Proof 6.1

(Proof of Proposition
5
)
.

From (1) to (2) is trivial.
We consider the inverse direction.
Assume
s,h\models{\rm ls}(t,u)*\phi
.
We will show
s,h\models\overrightarrow{\psi}
.

By the assumption, there are
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
such that

s,h_{1}\models{\rm ls}(t,u)
,
s,h_{2}\models\phi
, and
h=h_{1}+h_{2}
.
Hence
s,h_{1}\models{\rm ls}^{n}(t,u)
for some
n
(take the smallest one).

We will show
s,h\models\overrightarrow{\psi}
.
In the case of
n=0
, it is not the case since
{\rm ls}^{0}(t,u)
is unsatisfiable.
In the case of
n=1
or
n=3
, we have
s,h\models\overrightarrow{\psi}
by (2).

We consider the other cases.
Let
d
be the second component of
h_{1}(s(t))
.
Then the current situation of
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
is depicted as follows:

Formula

{\rm ls}(t,u)

*

\phi

Heap

Fix values

such that

by

h_{2}

Hence, by the assumption, we have

\begin{array}[]{l}s^{\prime},h^{\prime}_{1}+h_{2}\models\psi_{j}\end{array}

for some
\psi_{j}\in\overrightarrow{\psi}
.
Recall that
z,y_{1},y_{2}
do not appear in
\psi_{j}
since they are fresh variables.
So we have

\begin{array}[]{l}s,h^{\prime}_{1}+h_{2}\models\psi_{j}.\end{array}

Let
\psi_{j}\equiv\Pi\land\Sigma
.
Then
s\models\Pi
, and there are
h_{3}
,
h_{4}
and
an atomic spatial formula
\sigma
such that

s,h_{3}\models\sigma
,
s,h_{4}\models\Sigma-\sigma
,
a\in{\rm Dom}(h_{3})
, and
h_{3}+h_{4}=h^{\prime}_{1}+h_{2}
,
where
\Sigma-\sigma
is the spatial formula obtained by removing
\sigma
from
\Sigma
.

We can show that
\sigma
must have the form
{\rm ls}(p,q)
as follows:
it cannot be
p\mapsto(\hbox{-},\hbox{-})
by
a\neq s(p)
;
it cannot be
{\rm Arr}(p,q)
by
a\not\in[s(p),s(q)]
;
it cannot be
{\rm dll}(p,q,p^{\prime},q^{\prime})
, otherwise,
by
a\neq s(p),s(p^{\prime}),b
and
h_{3}(a)=(s(u),b)
,
we have
b\in{\rm Dom}(h_{3})\setminus\{a\}\subseteq{\rm Dom}(h_{3}+h_{4})\setminus\{a\}={\rm Dom}(h^{\prime}_{1}+h_{2})\setminus\{a\}\subseteq{\rm Dom}(h)
, which contradicts the condition of
b
.

Note that
a\neq s(p),s(q)
by the condition of
a
.
Hence we have

s(t),a\in{\rm Dom}(h_{3})
,

h_{3}(s(t))=h^{\prime}_{1}(s(t))=(a,d)

and
h_{3}(a)=h^{\prime}_{1}(a)=(s(u),b)
.
Therefore the current situation of
h_{3}
and
h_{4}
is depicted as follows:

Formula

{\rm ls}(p,q)

\Sigma-\sigma

+

h_{4}

Note that the above picture covers both cases of
n=2
and
n\geq 4
.
It satisfies
s,h^{\prime}_{3}\models{\rm ls}(p,q)
.
We have
h^{\prime}_{3}+h_{4}=h_{1}+h_{2}=h

by removing
h^{\prime}_{1}
from both sides of
h_{3}+h_{4}=h^{\prime}_{1}+h_{2}
and adding
h_{1}
to them.
Hence we have
s,h^{\prime}_{3}+h_{4}\models\Sigma
.
Therefore we have (1) since
s,h\models\overrightarrow{\psi}
can be obtained.

The unroll collapse property for the doubly-linked list predicate is given in the next proposition.
The key idea of its proof is similar to that of Proposition
5
:
First the list
{\rm dll}(t,u,v,w)
is replaced by some doubly-linked list

{\rm dll}(t,u,v,w)
of length 3 in the antecedent of (1), in order to show (1).
Then (2) applies to obtain the succedent. Then we can show that the
doubly-linked list of length 3 is contained in some list either
{\rm ls}(p,q)
or
{\rm dll}(p,q,p^{\prime},q^{\prime})

of the succedent. By replacing the doubly-linked list of length 3 by
{\rm dll}(t,u,v,w)
of arbitrary
length, we obtain the succedent of (1), since the difference by the
replacement is absorbed by
{\rm ls}(p,q)
or
{\rm dll}(p,q,p^{\prime},q^{\prime})
.

Proposition 6

(Unroll Collapse for
{\rm dll}
)
.

The following clauses are equivalent:

(1)

{\rm dll}(t,u,v,w)*\phi\models\overrightarrow{\psi}
.

(2)

t=u\land v=w\land\phi\models\overrightarrow{\psi}
,
t=v\land t\mapsto(u,w)*\phi\models\overrightarrow{\psi}
, and

t\mapsto(z,w)*z\mapsto(v,t)*v\mapsto(u,z)*\phi\models\overrightarrow{\psi}
,
where
z
is fresh.

Proof 6.2

(Proof of Proposition
6
)
.

From (1) to (2) is trivial.
We consider the inverse direction.
Assume
s,h\models{\rm dll}(t,u,v,w)*\phi
.
We will show
s,h\models\overrightarrow{\psi}
.

By the assumption, there are
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
such that

s,h_{1}\models{\rm dll}(t,u,v,w)
,

s,h_{2}\models\phi
, and

h=h_{1}+h_{2}
.
Hence
s,h_{1}\models{\rm dll}^{n}(t,u,v,w)
for some
n
(take the smallest one).

We will show
s,h\models\overrightarrow{\psi}
.
The case of
n=0
does not happen since
{\rm dll}^{0}(t,u,v,w)
is unsatisfiable.
In the case of
n=1
,
n=2
, or
n=4
, we have
s,h\models\overrightarrow{\psi}
by (2).

We consider the other cases.
The current situation of
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
is depicted as follows:

Formula

{\rm dll}(t,u,v,w)

*

\phi

+

h_{2}

Fix a value
a\in N
such that

a\not\in{\rm Dom}(h)\cup\{s(p),s(q),s(r)\mid p\mapsto(q,r)\hbox{ is in }\overrightarrow{\psi}\}\cup\{s(p),s(q)\mid{\rm ls}(p,q)\hbox{ is in }\overrightarrow{\psi}\}\cup\{s(p),s(q),s(p^{\prime}),s(q^{\prime})\mid{\rm dll}(p,q,p^{\prime},q^{\prime})\hbox{ is in }\overrightarrow{\psi}\}\cup\bigcup\{[s(p),s(q)]\mid{\rm Arr}(p,q)\hbox{ is in }\overrightarrow{\psi}\}
.

Define
h^{\prime}_{1}
by
{\rm Dom}(h^{\prime}_{1})=\{s(t),a,s(v)\}
,

h^{\prime}_{1}(m)=(a,s(w))
if
m=s(t)
,

h^{\prime}_{1}(m)=(s(v),s(t))
if
m=a
, and

h^{\prime}_{1}(m)=(s(u),a)
if
m=s(v)
.

Then we have

\begin{array}[]{l}s[z:=a],h^{\prime}_{1}+h_{2}\models t\mapsto(z,w)*z\mapsto(v,t)*v\mapsto(u,z)*\phi.\end{array}

+

h_{2}

Then, by the assumption, we have

\begin{array}[]{l}s,h^{\prime}_{1}+h_{2}\models\psi_{j}\end{array}

for some
\psi_{j}\in\overrightarrow{\psi}
,
since
z
does not appear in
\psi_{j}
.

Let
\psi_{j}\equiv\Pi\land\Sigma
.
Then
s\models\Pi
, and there are
h_{3}
,
h_{4}
and
\sigma
such that

s,h_{3}\models\sigma
,

s,h_{4}\models\Sigma-\sigma
,

a\in{\rm Dom}(h_{3})
, and

h_{3}+h_{4}=h^{\prime}_{1}+h_{2}
.

We can show
\sigma
have the form
{\rm ls}(p,q)
or
{\rm dll}(p,q,p^{\prime},q^{\prime})
as follows:
it cannot be
p\mapsto(\hbox{-},\hbox{-})
by
a\neq s(p)
;
it cannot be
{\rm Arr}(p,q)
by
a\not\in[s(p),s(q)]
.

Case 1:
the case of
\sigma\equiv{\rm dll}(p,q,p^{\prime},q^{\prime})
.
Note that
s(v),s(t),a\in{\rm Dom}(h_{3})
,
since
a
cannot be the first or last cell of the dll by
a\neq s(p),s(p^{\prime})
.
Hence

h_{3}(s(t))=h^{\prime}_{1}(s(t))=(a,s(w))

and

h_{3}(a)=h^{\prime}_{1}(a)=(s(v),s(t))
.
This case is depicted as follows:

Formula

{\rm dll}(p,q,p^{\prime},q^{\prime})

+

h_{4}

Note that the above picture covers both cases of
n=3
and
n\geq 5
.
It satisfies
s,h^{\prime}_{3}\models{\rm dll}(p,q,p^{\prime},q^{\prime})
.
We have
h^{\prime}_{3}+h_{4}=h_{1}+h_{2}=h

by removing
h^{\prime}_{1}
from both sides of
h_{3}+h_{4}=h^{\prime}_{1}+h_{2}
and adding
h_{1}
to them.
Hence we have
s,h^{\prime}_{3}+h_{4}\models\Sigma
.
Therefore we have (1) since
s,h\models\overrightarrow{\psi}
can be obtained.

Case 2:
the case of
\sigma\equiv{\rm ls}(p,q)
.
Note that
a,s(t)\in{\rm Dom}(h_{3})
,
since
a
cannot be the first cell of the list by
a\neq s(p)
.
Hence

h_{3}(s(t))=h^{\prime}_{1}(s(t))=(a,s(w))

and

h_{3}(a)=h^{\prime}_{1}(a)=(s(v),s(t))
.
We also note that
s(v)\in{\rm Dom}(h_{3})
or
s(v)\in{\rm Dom}(h_{4})
.
The latter case implies
s(v)=s(q)
.
We consider two subcases about where
s(v)
is.

Case 2.1:
the case of
s(v)\in{\rm Dom}(h_{3})
.
Consider
h^{\prime}_{3}
taken in the case 1.
This case is depicted as follows:

{\rm ls}(p,q)

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}_{h^{\prime}_{3}}

+

h_{4}

Note that the above picture covers both cases of
n=3
and
n\geq 5
.
It satisfies
s,h^{\prime}_{3}\models{\rm ls}(p,q)
.
We have
h^{\prime}_{3}+h_{4}=h_{1}+h_{2}=h

by removing
h^{\prime}_{1}
from both sides of
h_{3}+h_{4}=h^{\prime}_{1}+h_{2}
and adding
h_{1}
to them.
Hence we have
s,h^{\prime}_{3}+h_{4}\models\Sigma
.
Therefore we have (1) since
s,h\models\overrightarrow{\psi}
can be obtained.

Case 2.2:
the case of
s(v)\in{\rm Dom}(h_{4})
.
Recall that this case implies
s(v)=s(q)
.

otherwise.

if

, and

if
m=s(v)
.
